```python
                        # total_valid = 0
                        # L = len_x + len_y
                        # if len_x == len_y:
                        #     total_valid = 2
                        # elif len_x == len_y + 1:
                        #     total_valid = 1
                        # elif len_x == len_y - 1:
                        #     total_valid = 1
                        # else:
                        #     total_valid = 0
                        
                        # Then add total_valid to answer.
                    
                    # This gives the count for this substring pair
                    len_x = j - i + 1
                    len_y = l - k + 1
                    L = len_x + len_y
                    
                    # Count valid alternating sequences
                    valid_sequences = 0
                    
                    # Case 1: start with 0
                    # positions: 0,1,2,3,... with 0 at even indices, 1 at odd indices
                    # number of 0s = (L + 1) // 2
                    # number of 1s = L // 2
                    zeros_start_0 = (L + 1) // 2
                    ones_start_0 = L // 2
                    
                    if zeros_start_0 == len_x and ones_start_0 == len_y:
                        valid_sequences += 1
                    
                    # Case 2: start with 1  
                    # positions: 0,1,2,3,... with 1 at even indices, 0 at odd indices
                    # number of 0s = L // 2
                    # number of 1s = (L + 1) // 2
                    zeros_start_1 = L // 2
                    ones_start_1 = (L + 1) // 2
                    
                    if zeros_start_1 == len_x and ones_start_1 == len_y:
                        valid_sequences += 1
                    
                    total = (total + valid_sequences) % MOD
    
    print(total)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings x and y
2. For each substring of x and each substring of y, we need to count the number of "chaotic" merging sequences
3. A merging sequence produces a string z by alternately taking characters from the front of x or y
4. A merge is "chaotic" if no two consecutive characters in z are the same
5. We need to sum this count over all possible substring pairs

## Key Insights

1. For a merge to be chaotic, no two consecutive characters in the resulting string z can be the same
2. This means if we have a sequence of operations (0s and 1s), the resulting characters must alternate
3. The resulting character at each step depends on which string we're taking from (x or y)
4. For a merge to be chaotic, if we take a character from x at step i, the next character must come from a different string (so the next character is from the other string)

## Approach

For each possible substring of x and each possible substring of y:
1. We need to check if there exists a valid merging sequence that produces a chaotic merge
2. The key insight is that for a sequence to be chaotic, the positions where we take from x and y must alternate

Let me think about when a merge can be chaotic:
- If we take from x at position i, then at position i+1 we must take from y (different string)
- If we take from y at position i, then at position i+1 we must take from x (different string)

This means the sequence of operations must alternate: 0,1,0,1,... or 1,0,1,0,...

So for a valid chaotic merge, the operations must alternate between 0 and 1.

## Algorithm

For each substring of x and each substring of y:
1. We can form a merge by alternating between the two strings
2. For alternating sequence to work, we need to check that the resulting string doesn't have consecutive identical characters
3. The resulting characters are: x[i1], y[i2], x[i3], y[i4], ... or y[i1], x[i2], y[i3], x[i4], ...

We need to count valid alternating sequences that produce chaotic merges.

Actually, let me reconsider. A merge is chaotic if for any consecutive positions, the characters are different. This happens naturally when we alternate between taking from different strings, but we also need to ensure that the actual characters we take are different.

Wait, no - the condition is only on the characters in the resulting string, not on which string we took from.

For a merge to be chaotic, we need z[i] ≠ z[i+1] for all i.

So if we have a sequence of operations, the resulting string z depends on which characters we take from x and y. The constraint is that no two consecutive characters in z are the same.

## Simpler Approach

For a given substring of x and substring of y, we need to count the number of ways to interleave them such that no two consecutive characters in the result are the same.

The key insight is that for the merge to be chaotic, we must have alternating operations: 0,1,0,1,... or 1,0,1,0,...

So we only need to consider alternating sequences.

For each pair of substrings:
- Try alternating starting with x (0,1,0,1,...)
- Try alternating starting with y (1,0,1,0,...)
- For each pattern, check if the resulting string is chaotic
- Count valid patterns

```python
def solve():
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    MOD = 998244353
    n = len(x)
    m = len(y)
    
    total = 0
    
    # For each substring of x
    for i1 in range(n):
        for j1 in range(i1, n):
            # For each substring of y
            for i2 in range(m):
                for j2 in range(i2, m):
                    # Current substrings
                    sub_x = x[i1:j1+1]
                    sub_y = y[i2:j2+1]
                    
                    len_x = len(sub_x)
                    len_y = len(sub_y)
                    
                    # Count valid merging sequences for this pair
                    count = 0
                    
                    # Try alternating starting with x (0,1,0,1,...)
                    if len_x == 0 or len_y == 0:
                        continue
                        
                    # Check if we can alternate starting with x
                    if len_x + len_y > 0:
                        # Try sequence starting with x
                        merged = []
                        x_idx = 0
                        y_idx = 0
                        
                        # We need to take all characters
                        # For alternating pattern starting with x: x, y, x, y, ...
                        # For alternating pattern starting with y: y, x, y, x, ...
                        
                        # Pattern 1: start with x
                        if len_x > 0 and len_y > 0:
                            # We can alternate properly only if we don't have consecutive characters in the result
                            # But actually, we need to check the resulting string
                            
                            # Try the alternating pattern starting with x
                            chars = []
                            i, j = 0, 0
                            
                            # This is the alternating pattern
                            for k in range(len_x + len_y):
                                if k % 2 == 0:
                                    if i < len_x:
                                        chars.append(sub_x[i])
                                        i += 1
                                    else:
                                        # Should not happen in valid alternating
                                        break
                                else:
                                    if j < len_y:
                                        chars.append(sub_y[j])
                                        j += 1
                                    else:
                                        # Should not happen in valid alternating
                                        break
                            
                            # Check if this produces a chaotic string
                            is_chaotic = True
                            for k in range(len(chars) - 1):
                                if chars[k] == chars[k+1]:
                                    is_chaotic = False
                                    break
                            
                            if is_chaotic:
                                count += 1
                        
                        # Pattern 2: start with y
                        if len_x > 0 and len_y > 0:
                            chars = []
                            i, j = 0, 0
                            
                            for k in range(len_x + len_y):
                                if k % 2 == 0:
                                    if j < len_y:
                                        chars.append(sub_y[j])
                                        j += 1
                                    else:
                                        break
                                else:
                                    if i < len_x:
                                        chars.append(sub_x[i])
                                        i += 1
                                    else:
                                        break
                            
                            # Check if this produces a chaotic string
                            is_chaotic = True
                            for k in range(len(chars) - 1):
                                if chars[k] == chars[k+1]:
                                    is_chaotic = False
                                    break
                            
                            if is_chaotic:
                                count += 1
                    
                    # If both substrings are empty, it's not valid
                    if len_x == 0 and len_y == 0:
                        continue
                    
                    # If we have just one string
                    if len_x == 1 and len_y == 0:
                        # Only one way: take from x
                        # This produces a single character, so no consecutive pairs
                        count += 1
                    elif len_x == 0 and len_y == 1:
                        count += 1
                    
                    total = (total + count) % MOD
    
    print(total)

solve()
```

Wait, I think I'm overcomplicating this. Let me reconsider.

Actually, the problem is asking for the number of different merging sequences that produce chaotic merges. A merging sequence is defined by the 0s and 1s.

For a given pair of substrings, we need to count how many interleavings (sequences of 0s and 1s) produce a chaotic merge.

But the constraint is that the resulting string z must be chaotic (no consecutive identical characters).

For the sequence to be chaotic, we need z[i] ≠ z[i+1] for all i.

Let me implement a cleaner solution:

```python
def solve():
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    MOD = 998244353
    n = len(x)
    m = len(y)
    
    total = 0
    
    # For each substring of x
    for i1 in range(n):
        for j1 in range(i1, n):
            # For each substring of y
            for i2 in range(m):
                for j2 in range(i2, m):
                    sub_x = x[i1:j1+1]
                    sub_y = y[i2:j2+1]
                    len_x = len(sub_x)
                    len_y = len(sub_y)
                    
                    # Count valid merging sequences
                    valid_sequences = 0
                    
                    # Try all possible interleavings that maintain the order
                    # For an interleaving to be valid, we need to traverse all characters
                    
                    # We'll use dynamic programming or simply generate all possible sequences
                    # But since |sub_x| + |sub_y| <= 1000, we can't enumerate all possibilities
                    # Instead, we need to think about when the merge is chaotic
                    
                    # A merge is chaotic if no two consecutive characters are the same
                    # This means if we take character from x at position i and y at position j,
                    # then we need the characters to alternate properly
                    
                    # For the sequence to be chaotic, the characters must alternate, so the operations must alternate
                    # So we only need to consider alternating patterns
                    
                    if len_x == 0:
                        # Only from y
                        if len_y == 0:
                            continue
                        # We can only take from y, so it's just one sequence
                        # This produces a string with no consecutive characters, so it's chaotic
                        valid_sequences = 1
                    elif len_y == 0:
                        # Only from x
                        if len_x == 0:
                            continue
                        valid_sequences = 1
                    else:
                        # Both non-empty
                        # We can have two alternating patterns
                        
                        # Pattern 1: start with x
                        # Pattern 2: start with y
                        
                        # Try pattern starting with x
                        pattern1 = []
                        x_idx, y_idx = 0, 0
                        for pos in range(len_x + len_y):
                            if pos % 2 == 0:
                                if x_idx < len_x:
                                    pattern1.append(sub_x[x_idx])
                                    x_idx += 1
                                else:
                                    break
                            else:
                                if y_idx < len_y:
                                    pattern1.append(sub_y[y_idx])
                                    y_idx += 1
                                else:
                                    break
                        
                        # Check if pattern1 is chaotic
                        if len(pattern1) > 1:
                            is_chaotic = True
                            for i in range(len(pattern1) - 1):
                                if pattern1[i] == pattern1[i+1]:
                                    is_chaotic = False
                                    break
                            if is_chaotic:
                                valid_sequences += 1
                        
                        # Try pattern starting with y
                        pattern2 = []
                        x_idx, y_idx = 0, 0
                        for pos in range(len_x + len_y):
                            if pos % 2 == 0:
                                if y_idx < len_y:
                                    pattern2.append(sub_y[y_idx])
                                    y_idx += 1
                                else:
                                    break
                            else:
                                if x_idx < len_x:
                                    pattern2.append(sub_x[x_idx])
                                    x_idx += 1
                                else:
                                    break
                        
                        # Check if pattern2 is chaotic
                        if len(pattern2) > 1:
                            is_chaotic = True
                            for i in range(len(pattern2) - 1):
                                if pattern2[i] == pattern2[i+1]:
                                    is_chaotic = False
                                    break
                            if is_chaotic:
                                valid_sequences += 1
                    
                    total = (total + valid_sequences) % MOD
    
    print(total)

solve()
```

I think there's still an issue. The problem states "different merging sequences" and they are defined by the 0s and 1s. In my solution, I'm only considering the alternating patterns. But there might be other interleavings where we take from the same string consecutively, as long as the resulting characters don't match consecutively.

Wait, no. If we take from the same string consecutively, we might get the same character again, making it non-chaotic. But actually, the condition is only on the resulting characters, not on which string we took from.

For example, if x = "aa", y = "b", and we take "001", we get "a" + "a" + "b" = "aab", which is not chaotic because 'a' == 'a'.

So the sequence must ensure that consecutive characters in z are different.

But we also have to consider all possible interleavings, not just the alternating ones.

However, if we take from the same string twice in a row (like 0,0 or 1,1), then we're taking two characters from the same string consecutively, which might cause the resulting string to have consecutive identical characters.

Actually, if we take from x at step i and x at step i+1, we get x[j] and x[k] where j and k are indices in x. These might be equal or not.

So we need to check all possible interleavings.

But the number of interleavings is C(len_x + len_y, len_x), which can be huge.

For |x| and |y| up to 1000, the substrings can have up to 1000 characters, so the interleaving count is astronomical.

This suggests my initial interpretation is wrong.

Let me re-read the problem.

"A merge z is produced from a sequence a by the following rules: if a_i=0, then remove a letter from the beginning of x and append it to the end of z; if a_i=1, then remove a letter from the beginning of y and append it to the end of z."

So the merge z is formed by taking characters from x or y in the order specified by sequence a.

A sequence a is a merging sequence if it has exactly |x[l1,r1]| zeros and |y[l2,r2]| ones.

For the merge to be chaotic, no two consecutive characters in z should be equal.

The key insight: For the merge to be chaotic, we need z[i] ≠ z[i+1] for all i.

So when we take a character from x or y, we need to ensure it's different from the previous character.

In particular, if we take a character from x and the previous character was also from x, then we need the characters to be different.

Similarly for y.

So for a given pair of substrings, we can use dynamic programming to count the number of valid sequences.

Let me implement a DP solution for each substring pair:

```python
def solve():
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    MOD = 998244353
    n = len(x)
    m = len(y)
    
    total = 0
    
    # For each substring of x
    for i1 in range(n):
        for j1 in range(i1, n):
            # For each substring of y
            for i2 in range(m):
                for j2 in range(i2, m):
                    sub_x = x[i1:j1+1]
                    sub_y = y[i2:j2+1]
                    len_x = len(sub_x)
                    len_y = len(sub_y)
                    
                    if len_x == 0 and len_y == 0:
                        continue
                    
                    # DP: dp[i][j][last_char][last_from] = count of ways
                    # i - positions processed in sub_x
                    # j - positions processed in sub_y
                    # last_char - the last character in z (or None if none)
                    # last_from - which string the last character came from (0 for x, 1 for y)
                    
                    # We only care about characters, so we can reduce state
                    from collections import defaultdict
                    
                    # We'll use: dp[i][j][prev_char][prev_from]
                    # prev_char is the character (or -1 if none)
                    # prev_from is 0 for x, 1 for y
                    
                    # Initialize
                    dp = {}
                    
                    # Start state: no characters in z
                    # We can start by taking from x or y
                    
                    # If we start with x
                    if len_x > 0:
                        dp[(0, 0, -1, -1)] = 1
                    
                    # If we start with y  
                    if len_y > 0:
                        dp[(0, 0, -1, -1)] = 1  # Already covered
                    
                    # Process all positions
                    for x_idx in range(len_x):
                        for y_idx in range(len_y):
                            new_dp = {}
                            for (ix, iy, last_char, last_from), count in dp.items():
                                # Try to take from x, if available
                                if ix < len_x:
                                    new_char = sub_x[ix]
                                    if last_char == -1:
                                        # First character
                                        new_dp[(ix+1, iy, new_char, 0)] = (new_dp.get((ix+1, iy, new_char, 0), 0) + count) % MOD
                                    else:
                                        # Check if new_char != last_char
                                        if new_char != last_char:
                                            new_dp[(ix+1, iy, new_char, 0)] = (new_dp.get((ix+1, iy, new_char, 0), 0) + count) % MOD
                                
                                # Try to take from y, if available
                                if iy < len_y:
                                    new_char = sub_y[iy]
                                    if last_char == -1:
                                        new_dp[(ix, iy+1, new_char, 1)] = (new_dp.get((ix, iy+1, new_char, 1), 0) + count) % MOD
                                    else:
                                        if new_char != last_char:
                                            new_dp[(ix, iy+1, new_char, 1)] = (new_dp.get((ix, iy+1, new_char, 1), 0) + count) % MOD
                            
                            dp = new_dp
                    
                    # Sum all valid endings
                    valid_count = 0
                    for (ix, iy, last_char, last_from), count in dp.items():
                        if ix == len_x and iy == len_y:
                            valid_count = (valid_count + count) % MOD
                    
                    total = (total + valid_count) % MOD
    
    print(total)

solve()
```

This solution is O(|x|² × |y|² × |x| × |y|) which is too slow for |x|,|y| ≤ 1000.

I need to optimize. The state space is O(|x| × |y| × 26 × 2) which is about 1000 × 1000 × 52 = 52 million, which might be too much.

But the recurrence is O(|x| × |y|) per state, and there are O(|x| × |y|) states, so total O(|x|² × |y|²) which is (10^6)² = 10^12, which is too slow.

I think I need to reconsider the problem. Let me look at the examples.

In the first example: "aaa", "bb"

The answer is 24.

After re-thinking, I believe the alternating pattern approach might be correct because if we take from the same string consecutively, we might get the same character twice.

But let me check the constraint more carefully.

Actually, I think there's a mathematical insight: for the merge to be chaotic, the operations must alternate between taking from x and y. This is because if we take from x twice in a row, we get x[i] and x[j], and if i and j are such that x[i] == x[j], it's not chaotic.

Similarly for y.

But even if x[i] != x[j], we still have to ensure the resulting characters are different.

However, in the worst case, it's still expensive.

Given the constraints and that this is a competitive programming problem, there must be a simpler insight.

After further thought, I believe the intended solution is that the only way to get a chaotic merge is to alternate between the two strings. This is because if you take from the same string twice in a row, you're essentially concatenating two characters from that string in a row.

But the problem doesn't require that the characters from the same string be different - only that consecutive characters in the resulting string are different.

For example, x="ab", y="c" could have interleaving "01" -> "a"+"c" = "ac" (chaotic), or "10" -> "c"+"a" = "ca" (chaotic), or "00" -> "a"+"a" = "aa" (not chaotic).

So only the alternating patterns might work.

In the example with "aaa", "bb", let's verify:

- Substrings: "a" with "b" -> "01" gives "a"+"b" = "ab" (chaotic), "10" gives "b"+"a" = "ba" (chaotic) -> 2 ways
- "a" with "bb" -> "010" gives "a"+"b"+"b" = "abb" (not chaotic), "101" gives "b"+"a"+"b" = "bab" (chaotic) -> 1 way
- "aa" with "b" -> "010" gives "a"+"b"+"a" = "aba" (chaotic), "101" gives "b"+"a"+"b" = "bab" (chaotic) -> 2 ways
- "aa" with "bb" -> "0101" gives "a"+"b"+"a"+"b" = "abab" (chaotic), "1010" gives "b"+"a"+"b"+"a" = "baba" (chaotic) -> 2 ways
- "aaa" with "b" -> "010" gives "a"+"b"+"a" = "aba" (chaotic), "101" gives "b"+"a"+"b" = "bab" (chaotic) -> but wait, this is only for length 3 and 1
- "aaa" with "bb" -> "01010" gives "a"+"b"+"a"+"b"+"a" = "ababa" (chaotic), "10101" gives "b"+"a"+"b"+"a"+"b" = "babab" (chaotic) -> 2 ways

This doesn't match the provided count in the note.

I think I need to implement the DP with optimization, even if it's slow.

```python
def solve():
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    MOD = 998244353
    
    n = len(x)
    m = len(y)
    
    total = 0
    
    # For each substring of x
    for i1 in range(n):
        for j1 in range(i1, n):
            sub_x = x[i1:j1+1]
            len_x = len(sub_x)
            
            # For each substring of y
            for i2 in range(m):
                for j2 in range(i2, m):
                    sub_y = y[i2:j2+1]
                    len_y = len(sub_y)
                    
                    if len_x == 0 and len_y == 0:
                        continue
                    
                    # DP: dp[x_pos][y_pos][last_char][last_from]
                    # last_char: character, or -1 if none
                    # last_from: 0 for x, 1 for y
                    
                    # State: (x_pos, y_pos, last_char, last_from)
                    dp = {}
                    
                    # Initial state: no characters selected
                    # We can start by selecting from x or y
                    if len_x > 0:
                        dp[(0, 0, -1, -1)] = 1
                    if len_y > 0:
                        dp[(0, 0, -1, -1)] = 1  # already handled
                    
                    # Fill DP table
                    for x_pos in range(len_x):
                        for y_pos in range(len_y):
                            new_dp = {}
                            for (px, py, last_char, last_from), count in dp.items():
                                # Try to take from x (if possible)
                                if px < len_x:
                                    char = sub_x[px]
                                    if last_char == -1:
                                        new_dp[(px+1, py, char, 0)] = (new_dp.get((px+1, py, char, 0), 0) + count) % MOD
                                    else:
                                        if char != last_char:
                                            new_dp[(px+1, py, char, 0)] = (new_dp.get((px+1, py, char, 0), 0) + count) % MOD
                                
                                # Try to take from y (if possible)
                                if py < len_y:
                                    char = sub_y[py]
                                    if last_char == -1:
                                        new_dp[(px, py+1, char, 1)] = (new_dp.get((px, py+1, char, 1), 0) + count) % MOD
                                    else:
                                        if char != last_char:
                                            new_dp[(px, py+1, char, 1)] = (new_dp.get((px, py+1, char, 1), 0) + count) % MOD
                            
                            dp = new_dp
                    
                    # Count valid sequences that complete both substrings
                    count = 0
                    for (px, py, last_char, last_from), c in dp.items():
                        if px == len_x and py == len_y:
                            count = (count + c) % MOD
                    
                    total = (total + count) % MOD
    
    print(total)

solve()
```

This might be too slow, but it should be correct. Let me optimize the state representation.

```python
def solve():
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    MOD = 998244353
    
    n = len(x)
    m = len(y)
    
    total = 0
    
    # For each substring of x
    for i1 in range(n):
        for j1 in range(i1, n):
            sub_x = x[i1:j1+1]
            len_x = len(sub_x)
            
            # For each substring of y
            for i2 in range(m):
                for j2 in range(i2, m):
                    sub_y = y[i2:j2+1]
                    len_y = len(sub_y)
                    
                    if len_x == 0 and len_y == 0:
                        continue
                    
                    # DP: dp[x_pos][y_pos][last_char][last_from]
                    # Use dictionary for state
                    from collections import defaultdict
                    
                    dp = defaultdict(int)
                    # State: (x_pos, y_pos, last_char, last_from)
                    # last_char: char or -1 (special value)
                    # last_from: 0 or 1
                    
                    # Start state
                    if len_x > 0:
                        dp[(0, 0, -1, -1)] = 1
                    if len_y > 0:
                        dp[(0, 0, -1, -1)] = 1  # already set by first condition
                    
                    # Iterate through all positions
                    for x_pos in range(len_x):
                        for y_pos in range(len_y):
                            new_dp = defaultdict(int)
                            for (px, py, last_char, last_from), count in dp.items():
                                # Take from x
                                if px < len_x:
                                    c = sub_x[px]
                                    if last_char == -1:
                                        new_dp[(px+1, py, c, 0)] = (new_dp[(px+1, py, c, 0)] + count) % MOD
                                    else:
                                        if c != last_char:
                                            new_dp[(px+1, py, c, 0)] = (new_dp[(px+1, py, c, 0)] + count) % MOD
                                
                                # Take from y
                                if py < len_y:
                                    c = sub_y[py]
                                    if last_char == -1:
                                        new_dp[(px, py+1, c, 1)] = (new_dp[(px, py+1, c, 1)] + count) % MOD
                                    else:
                                        if c != last_char:
                                            new_dp[(px, py+1, c, 1)] = (new_dp[(px, py+1, c, 1)] + count) % MOD
                            
                            dp = new_dp
                    
                    # Sum valid final states
                    res = 0
                    for (px, py, last_char, last_from), count in dp.items():
                        if px == len_x and py == len_y:
                            res = (res + count) % MOD
                    
                    total = (total + res) % MOD
    
    print(total)

solve()
```

This should work, though it might be slow for the worst case. Given the constraints, it might pass in PyPy or with optimizations.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given two strings x and y, both consist only of lowercase Latin letters. Let |s| be the length of string s.

Let's call a sequence a a merging sequence if it consists of exactly |x| zeros and exactly |y| ones in some order.

A merge z is produced from a sequence a by the following rules: 

  * if a_i=0, then remove a letter from the beginning of x and append it to the end of z; 
  * if a_i=1, then remove a letter from the beginning of y and append it to the end of z. 



Two merging sequences a and b are different if there is some position i such that a_i ≠ b_i.

Let's call a string z chaotic if for all i from 2 to |z| z_{i-1} ≠ z_i.

Let s[l,r] for some 1 ≤ l ≤ r ≤ |s| be a substring of consecutive letters of s, starting from position l and ending at position r inclusive.

Let f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) be the number of different merging sequences of x[l_1,r_1] and y[l_2,r_2] that produce chaotic merges. Note that only non-empty substrings of x and y are considered.

Calculate ∑ _{1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| \\\ 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y|} f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2). Output the answer modulo 998 244 353.

Input

The first line contains a string x (1 ≤ |x| ≤ 1000).

The second line contains a string y (1 ≤ |y| ≤ 1000).

Both strings consist only of lowercase Latin letters.

Output

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings x and y
2. We need to consider all possible substrings of x and y
3. For each pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]), we need to find how many "merging sequences" produce a "chaotic" merge
4. A merging sequence is a sequence of 0s and 1s where 0 means take from front of x substring and 1 means take from front of y substring
5. A merge is chaotic if no two consecutive characters are the same
6. We need to sum f(l1,r1,l2,r2) over all possible substring pairs

## Key Insights

1. A merge is chaotic if we never have two consecutive characters that are the same
2. For a merging sequence to produce a chaotic merge, when we take characters from x and y, the resulting string must alternate between different characters
3. The merge starts with either a character from x (when we have a 0) or from y (when we have a 1)

## Approach

1. For each possible pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]):
   - We need to find all valid merging sequences that produce chaotic merges
   - A valid merging sequence produces a chaotic merge if no two consecutive characters in the result are the same
   - This means if the last character in the current merge was 'a', the next character must be something else

2. Key observation: For a merge to be chaotic, we need to alternate between characters. So if the first character is from x, the next must be from y, and so on.

3. However, there's a constraint: if we have a substring from x and a substring from y, and we want to merge them chaotically, the characters must alternate.

4. Actually, let me think about this more carefully:
   - If we have a sequence of 0s and 1s, it represents taking from x or y
   - The resulting merge is chaotic if no two consecutive positions have the same character
   - This means if we take from x at step i and from y at step i+1, the characters x[i] and y[i+1] must be different

5. But wait - the problem says "chaotic" means z[i-1] ≠ z[i] for all i from 2 to |z|. So consecutive characters in the result must be different.

6. Therefore, for a sequence of operations to produce a chaotic merge, whenever we take from x and then from y (or vice versa), the resulting characters must be different.

## Algorithm

For each pair of substrings:
1. Generate all possible merging sequences (sequences with exactly |x_sub| zeros and |y_sub| ones)
2. For each sequence, simulate the merge and check if it's chaotic
3. Count valid sequences

But this would be O(2^(n+m)) which is too slow.

Alternative insight:
- A merging sequence produces a chaotic merge if and only if for every transition between operations, the character from the source string is different from the previous character in the merge result.

Actually, let me reconsider: the chaos condition is on the final merge string, not on the operations.

So if we have operations: 0, 1, 0, 1, ...
- The merge gets: x[0], y[1], x[2], y[3], ...
- For chaos: x[0] ≠ y[1] AND y[1] ≠ x[2] AND x[2] ≠ y[3] AND ...

For transitions between operations (0→1 or 1→0), the character from x and the character from y must be different.

This suggests that a valid merging sequence exists only if we can alternate such that all adjacent characters in the result are different.

However, there's one more constraint: we need to consider all possible sequences, not just alternating ones.

Actually, I think the key insight is: for a merge to be chaotic, whenever we have two consecutive operations that result in characters, those characters must be different.

So we need to check if there exists a valid interleaving.

But the problem asks for the number of different merging sequences that produce chaotic merges, not just if at least one exists.

Given the complexity, I'll implement a dynamic programming solution that for each pair of substrings, counts the valid merging sequences.

## Solution Approach

For each pair of substrings (sub_x, sub_y):
- Use DP where state is (i, j, last_char) 
- i: number of characters taken from sub_x so far
- j: number of characters taken from sub_y so far
- last_char: the last character in the merge (or None if empty)

Transition:
- If we take from sub_x (operation 0): new_char = sub_x[i], check if new_char != last_char
- If we take from sub_y (operation 1): new_char = sub_y[j], check if new_char != last_char

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0
    
    # For each possible substring of x
    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]
            len_x = len(sub_x)
            
            # For each possible substring of y
            for l2 in range(m):
                for r2 in range(l2, m):
                    sub_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                    len_y = len(sub_y)
                    
                    # Count valid merging sequences for this pair
                    if len_x == 0 and len_y == 0:
                        total = (total + 1) % MOD
                    elif len_x == 0:
                        # Only from y
                        if len_y == 1:
                            # Only one character - no consecutive pairs, so valid
                            total = (total + 1) % MOD
                        else:
                            # Multiple characters - but only one merging sequence
                            # It's chaotic only if no consecutive characters are same
                            # But if all characters are same, it's not chaotic
                            if len_y == 1:
                                total = (total + 1) % MOD
                            else:
                                # For multiple characters, if all are same, not chaotic
                                # But we only have one sequence for a given length
                                # Actually, with only y, we have one sequence: 1,1,1,...
                                # And this produces the string sub_y
                                # For it to be chaotic, no two consecutive chars are equal
                                # So if sub_y has all same characters, it's not chaotic
                                char_count = {}
                                for c in sub_y:
                                    char_count[c] = char_count.get(c, 0) + 1
                                if len(char_count) == 1:
                                    # All characters are same - not chaotic
                                    pass
                                else:
                                    # Not all same - need to check if chaotic
                                    # Actually, if it has different characters, but consecutive might still be same
                                    # Need to check if adjacent chars in sub_y are different
                                    is_chaotic = True
                                    for i in range(1, len_y):
                                        if sub_y[i] == sub_y[i-1]:
                                            is_chaotic = False
                                            break
                                    if is_chaotic:
                                        total = (total + 1) % MOD
                    elif len_y == 0:
                        # Only from x
                        if len_x == 1:
                            total = (total + 1) % MOD
                        else:
                            # Check if chaotic
                            is_chaotic = True
                            for i in range(1, len_x):
                                if sub_x[i] == sub_x[i-1]:
                                    is_chaotic = False
                                    break
                            if is_chaotic:
                                total = (total + 1) % MOD
                    else:
                        # Both non-empty
                        # Use DP to count valid sequences
                        # dp[i][j][last] = number of ways to take i from x, j from y, ending with char 'last'
                        # last can be -1 (no previous), or a char
                        
                        # We can optimize by noting that we only care about valid transitions
                        # State: (i, j, last_char)
                        # last_char can be one of the characters in x or y, or -1
                        
                        # Initialize
                        dp = {}
                        
                        # Empty sequence
                        dp[(0, 0, -1)] = 1
                        
                        # Process all possible operations
                        i, j = 0, 0
                        while i < len_x or j < len_y:
                            new_dp = {}
                            
                            for (cur_i, cur_j, last), count in dp.items():
                                # Try taking from x if possible
                                if cur_i < len_x:
                                    new_char = sub_x[cur_i]
                                    # Check if this transition is valid (different from last)
                                    if last == -1 or new_char != last:
                                        new_key = (cur_i + 1, cur_j, new_char)
                                        new_dp[new_key] = (new_dp.get(new_key, 0) + count) % MOD
                                
                                # Try taking from y if possible
                                if cur_j < len_y:
                                    new_char = sub_y[cur_j]
                                    if last == -1 or new_char != last:
                                        new_key = (cur_i, cur_j + 1, new_char)
                                        new_dp[new_key] = (new_dp.get(new_key, 0) + count) % MOD
                            
                            # If we have any valid states, update dp
                            dp = new_dp
                            
                            # If we've processed all characters, break
                            if len(dp) == 0:
                                break
                            
                            # Move to next iteration
                            # We need to continue until no more moves
                            # Actually, we should just check if we can still make moves
                            if i == len_x and j == len_y:
                                break
                            
                            # Update i, j for the next iteration
                            i, j = cur_i, cur_j
                            
                            # This approach is flawed - we need to process all states
                            
                            # Let me restart - we need to process all possible sequences
                            
                            # Actually, we should just iterate through all possible states
                            # without fixing i,j
                            break
                        
                        # Correct DP approach
                        # Let me rewrite the DP properly
                        
                        dp = {}
                        dp[(0, 0, -1)] = 1
                        
                        # We need to process all possible states
                        # Since the state space is small (at most len_x * len_y), we can iterate
                        # through all possible next states from current states
                        
                        while True:
                            new_dp = {}
                            processed = False
                            
                            for (i_curr, j_curr, last), count in dp.items():
                                # Try to take from x
                                if i_curr < len_x:
                                    new_char = sub_x[i_curr]
                                    if last == -1 or new_char != last:
                                        new_state = (i_curr + 1, j_curr, new_char)
                                        new_dp[new_state] = (new_dp.get(new_state, 0) + count) % MOD
                                
                                # Try to take from y
                                if j_curr < len_y:
                                    new_char = sub_y[j_curr]
                                    if last == -1 or new_char != last:
                                        new_state = (i_curr, j_curr + 1, new_char)
                                        new_dp[new_state] = (new_dp.get(new_state, 0) + count) % MOD
                            
                            # If no new states, we're done
                            if len(new_dp) == 0:
                                break
                            
                            dp = new_dp
                            processed = True
                            
                            # If we've consumed all characters, we're done
                            if len_x == i_curr and len_y == j_curr:
                                break
                            
                        # The answer for this substring pair is the total count of valid sequences
                        total = (total + sum(dp.values()) % MOD) % MOD
    
    print(total)

solve()
```

```python
                                is_chaotic = False
                                break

                                        changed = True

                            if not changed:
                                break
                            
                            dp = new_dp
```

```python
                                    new_dp[new_key] = (new_dp.get(new_key, 0) + count) % MOD
```
                            
```python
                            # Try to take from y if possible
                            if j < len_y:
                                char_y = sub_y[j]
                                if last == -1 or char_y != last:
                                    new_key = (i, j + 1, char_y)
                                    new_dp[new_key] = (new_dp.get(new_key, 0) + count) % MOD
                        
                        # If no new states, we're done
                        if len(new_dp) == 0:
                            break
                            
                        dp = new_dp
                        
                        # If all characters are used, we're done
                        # This is a bit tricky - we should break when we can't make more moves
                        # but the loop above should terminate naturally
                        
                    # Add the total count of valid sequences
                    total = (total + sum(dp.values()) % MOD) % MOD

print(total)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given two strings x and y, both consist only of lowercase Latin letters. Let |s| be the length of string s.

Let's call a sequence a a merging sequence if it consists of exactly |x| zeros and exactly |y| ones in some order.

A merge z is produced from a sequence a by the following rules: 

  * if a_i=0, then remove a letter from the beginning of x and append it to the end of z; 
  * if a_i=1, then remove a letter from the beginning of y and append it to the end of z. 



Two merging sequences a and b are different if there is some position i such that a_i ≠ b_i.

Let's call a string z chaotic if for all i from 2 to |z| z_{i-1} ≠ z_i.

Let s[l,r] for some 1 ≤ l ≤ r ≤ |s| be a substring of consecutive letters of s, starting from position l and ending at position r inclusive.

Let f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) be the number of different merging sequences of x[l_1,r_1] and y[l_2,r_2] that produce chaotic merges. Note that only non-empty substrings of x and y are considered.

Calculate ∑ _{1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| \\\ 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y|} f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2). Output the answer modulo 998 244 353.

Input

The first line contains a string x (1 ≤ |x| ≤ 1000).

The second line contains a string y (1 ≤ |y| ≤ 1000).

Both strings consist only of lowercase Latin letters.

Output

Print a single integer — the sum of f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) over 1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| and 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y| modulo 998 244 353.

  * 4 pairs of substrings "aa" and "b", each with a valid merging sequence "010"; 
  * 2 pairs of substrings "aa" and "bb", each with valid merging sequences "0101" and "1010"; 
  * 2 pairs of substrings "aaa" and "b", each with no valid merging sequences; 
  * 1 pair of substrings "aaa" and "bb" with a valid merging sequence "01010";

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings x and y, and we need to consider all possible substrings of both strings.
2. For each pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]), we need to find the number of "merging sequences" that produce a "chaotic" merge.
3. A merging sequence consists of 0s and 1s, where 0 means taking from the beginning of x and 1 means taking from the beginning of y.
4. A merge is chaotic if no two consecutive characters are the same.
5. We need to sum up f(l1,r1,l2,r2) over all possible substrings.

## Key Insights

1. For a merge to be chaotic, no two consecutive characters in the merge result should be the same.
2. The merge result alternates between characters from x and y based on the merging sequence.
3. A merging sequence is valid if and only if the resulting merge is chaotic.

## Approach

The key insight is that we need to count valid sequences that maintain alternation. 

For a merging sequence to produce a chaotic merge, the pattern must alternate strictly between characters from x and y. This means:
- If the first character comes from x, then the second must come from y, then from x, etc.
- If the first character comes from y, then the second must come from x, then from y, etc.

We can use dynamic programming with states:
- `dp[i][j][last][started]` where:
  - i is the current position in x substring
  - j is the current position in y substring  
  - last is the last character taken (0 for x, 1 for y, or -1 for not started)
  - started indicates if we've started the merge

But we need to optimize this since we have to consider all substring pairs.

Actually, let's think differently. For each pair of substrings, we can check if any merging sequence produces a chaotic merge.

## Detailed Solution

For a given pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]), we need to count valid merging sequences. A sequence is valid if no two consecutive characters in the merge are the same.

This means:
- If we take from x at position i, the character is x[i]
- If we take from y at position j, the character is y[j]
- The sequence alternates between x and y

So for each possible starting choice (0 from x or 1 from y), we can simulate the merge and check if it's chaotic.

We need to implement a function that for given substrings, counts valid merging sequences.

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0
    
    # For each possible substring of x
    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            # For each possible substring of y
            for l2 in range(m):
                for r2 in range(l2, m):
                    # Get the substrings
                    substr_x = x[l1:r1+1]
                    substr_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                    
                    # Count valid merging sequences
                    count = 0
                    
                    # Try all possible merging sequences
                    # We'll use DP to count valid sequences
                    # dp[i][j][last_char][alternating] where:
                    # i: position in x substring
                    # j: position in y substring
                    # last_char: what was the last character taken (0=x, 1=y, -1=none)
                    # alternating: current alternating pattern
                    
                    # Actually, simpler: for each possible starting position, simulate
                    # and check if all characters are different
                    
                    # Try starting with x
                    if substr_x and len(substr_x) + len(substr_y) > 0:
                        # Simulate merging starting with x
                        count_start_x = 0
                        # Use DP: dp[i][j][prev] where prev is -1, 0, or 1
                        # prev: -1 means no character yet, 0 means last was from x, 1 means last was from y
                        dp = {}
                        
                        def count_merge(i, j, last):
                            if i >= len(substr_x) and j >= len(substr_y):
                                return 1
                            
                            if (i, j, last) in dp:
                                return dp[(i, j, last)]
                            
                            result = 0
                            
                            # If we can take from x
                            if i < len(substr_x):
                                # Character from x
                                char_x = substr_x[i]
                                if last == -1 or last != 0:
                                    # Can take from x (stays within chaotic rule)
                                    result = (result + count_merge(i+1, j, 0)) % MOD
                            
                            # If we can take from y  
                            if j < len(substr_y):
                                char_y = substr_y[j]
                                if last == -1 or last != 1:
                                    # Can take from y
                                    result = (result + count_merge(i, j+1, 1)) % MOD
                            
                            dp[(i, j, last)] = result
                            return result
                        
                        count_start_x = count_merge(0, 0, -1)
                    
                    # Try starting with y
                    count_start_y = 0
                    if substr_y and len(substr_x) + len(substr_y) > 0:
                        dp = {}
                        
                        def count_merge(i, j, last):
                            if i >= len(substr_x) and j >= len(substr_y):
                                return 1
                            
                            if (i, j, last) in dp:
                                return dp[(i, j, last)]
                            
                            result = 0
                            
                            # If we can take from y
                            if j < len(substr_y):
                                char_y = substr_y[j]
                                if last == -1 or last != 1:
                                    result = (result + count_merge(i, j+1, 1)) % MOD
                            
                            # If we can take from x
                            if i < len(substr_x):
                                char_x = substr_x[i]
                                if last == -1 or last != 0:
                                    result = (result + count_merge(i+1, j, 0)) % MOD
                            
                            dp[(i, j, last)] = result
                            return result
                        
                        count_start_y = count_merge(0, 0, -1)
                    
                    # Total for this substring pair
                    valid_count = (count_start_x + count_start_y) % MOD
                    total = (total + valid_count) % MOD
    
    print(total)

solve()
```

Wait, I think there's an issue with my approach. Let me reconsider.

The problem is that the DP state is correct, but I need to be more careful about the implementation. Let me rewrite it more carefully:

```python
MOD = 998244353

x = input().strip()
y = input().strip()

n, m = len(x), len(y)
total = 0

# For each possible substring of x
for l1 in range(n):
    for r1 in range(l1, n):
        # For each possible substring of y
        for l2 in range(m):
            for r2 in range(l2, m):
                # Get the substrings
                substr_x = x[l1:r1+1]
                substr_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                
                # Count valid merging sequences
                count = 0
                
                # We need to check all possible merging sequences
                # Using DP: dp[i][j][last] where:
                # i: current position in x substring (0 to len(substr_x))
                # j: current position in y substring (0 to len(substr_y))
                # last: last character taken (0=x, 1=y, -1=none)
                
                # We'll use a 3D DP array
                # dp[i][j][last] = number of ways to reach (i,j) with last character
                from functools import lru_cache
                
                @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
                def dp(i, j, last):
                    # i: current position in x substring
                    # j: current position in y substring  
                    # last: -1 (none), 0 (last was from x), 1 (last was from y)
                    
                    # Base case: we've processed both strings
                    if i == len(substr_x) and j == len(substr_y):
                        return 1
                    
                    result = 0
                    
                    # Option 1: take from x (if possible and doesn't violate chaos)
                    if i < len(substr_x):
                        # Can we take from x?
                        if last == -1 or last != 0:
                            result = (result + dp(i+1, j, 0)) % MOD
                    
                    # Option 2: take from y (if possible and doesn't violate chaos)
                    if j < len(substr_y):
                        if last == -1 or last != 1:
                            result = (result + dp(i, j+1, 1)) % MOD
                    
                    return result
                
                # Start with no character taken
                count = dp(0, 0, -1)
                
                total = (total + count) % MOD

print(total)
```

                # Using DP: dp[i][j][last] where:

                # j: current position in y substring (0 to len(substr_y))
                # last: last character taken (0=x, 1=y, -1=none)

                        # Can we take from x?
                        if last == -1 or last != 0:
                            result = (result + dp(i+1, j, 0)) % MOD
                    
                    # Option 2: take from y (if possible and doesn't violate chaos)
                    if j < len(substr_y):
                        if last == -1 or last != 1:
                            result = (result + dp(i, j+1, 1)) % MOD
                    
                    return result
                
                # Start with no character taken
                count = dp(0, 0, -1)
                
                total = (total + count) % MOD

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings x and y
2. For each substring of x and each substring of y, we need to count the number of "chaotic" merging sequences
3. A merging sequence is formed by alternately taking characters from the beginning of the substring of x (when 0) or y (when 1)
4. A merge is "chaotic" if no two consecutive characters are the same
5. We need to sum this count over all possible substrings

## Key Insights

1. For a merging sequence to be chaotic, no two consecutive characters can be the same
2. When we have a sequence of 0s and 1s, it's chaotic only if no two adjacent elements are equal
3. This means we can only have alternating 0s and 1s (e.g., 0101... or 1010...)
4. The first move (0 or 1) determines the pattern
5. For a given substring of x and y, the number of valid merging sequences depends on whether the characters at each position match or not

## Algorithm Design

1. For each possible substring of x (from l1 to r1) and each possible substring of y (from l2 to r2):
   - Generate all possible merging sequences (all permutations of |sub_x| zeros and |sub_y| ones)
   - For each sequence, check if the resulting merge is chaotic
   - Count valid sequences

2. However, this would be too slow. We need to optimize.

## Optimization Insight

The key insight is that for a sequence to produce a chaotic merge:
- No two consecutive characters in the final string can be the same
- This means the merging sequence itself must alternate between 0 and 1
- So valid sequences are only those that alternate between 0 and 1
- For a substring of length m in x and n in y, the merging sequence must alternate

## Step-by-step Solution

1. For each pair of substrings (x[l1:r1+1], y[l2:r2+1]):
   - Calculate the length of each substring: len_x and len_y
   - The merging sequence has len_x zeros and len_y ones
   - For the merge to be chaotic, the sequence must alternate
   - This gives us exactly 2 valid sequences of the form 0101... or 1010...
   - But we need to check if these sequences actually produce chaotic merges

2. For a specific alternating sequence (e.g., 0101...), the merge is chaotic only if:
   - When we take a character from x (0), it's different from the previous character
   - When we take a character from y (1), it's different from the previous character

3. For alternating sequences, the merge will be chaotic if the alternating pattern works with the actual characters.

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0
    
    # For each substring of x
    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            # For each substring of y
            for l2 in range(m):
                for r2 in range(l2, m):
                    sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]
                    sub_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                    
                    len_x, len_y = len(sub_x), len(sub_y)
                    
                    # If either substring is empty, skip (but constraints say non-empty)
                    if len_x == 0 or len_y == 0:
                        continue
                    
                    # For chaotic merge, the sequence must alternate
                    # There are at most 2 valid alternating patterns: start with 0 or start with 1
                    valid_count = 0
                    
                    # Pattern 1: start with 0 (010101...)
                    pattern_0 = []
                    dest = 0
                    for i in range(len_x + len_y):
                        if dest % 2 == 0:
                            pattern_0.append(0)
                        else:
                            pattern_0.append(1)
                        dest += 1
                    
                    # Pattern 2: start with 1 (101010...)
                    pattern_1 = []
                    dest = 0
                    for i in range(len_x + len_y):
                        if dest % 2 == 0:
                            pattern_1.append(1)
                        else:
                            pattern_1.append(0)
                        dest += 1
                    
                    # Check if pattern_0 produces chaotic merge
                    merge_0 = []
                    i, j = 0, 0
                    for val in pattern_0:
                        if val == 0:
                            merge_0.append(sub_x[i])
                            i += 1
                        else:
                            merge_0.append(sub_y[j])
                            j += 1
                    
                    # Check if pattern_1 produces chaotic merge
                    merge_1 = []
                    i, j = 0, 0
                    for val in pattern_1:
                        if val == 0:
                            merge_1.append(sub_x[i])
                            i += 1
                        else:
                            merge_1.append(sub_y[j])
                            j += 1
                    
                    # Check if merge is chaotic - no consecutive same characters
                    chaotic_0 = True
                    for i in range(1, len(merge_0)):
                        if merge_0[i] == merge_0[i-1]:
                            chaotic_0 = False
                            break
                    
                    chaotic_1 = True
                    for i in range(1, len(merge_1)):
                        if merge_1[i] == merge_1[i-1]:
                            chaotic_1 = False
                            break
                    
                    if chaotic_0:
                        valid_count += 1
                    if chaotic_1:
                        valid_count += 1
                    
                    total = (total + valid_count) % MOD
    
    print(total)

solve()
```

```python
MOD = 998244353

x = input().strip()
y = input().strip()

n, m = len(x), len(y)
total = 0

# For each substring of x
for l1 in range(n):
    for r1 in range(l1, n):
        # For each substring of y
        for l2 in range(m):
            for r2 in range(l2, m):
                sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]
                sub_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                
                len_x, len_y = len(sub_x), len(sub_y)
                
                # If either substring is empty, skip
                if len_x == 0 or len_y == 0:
                    continue
                
                # For chaotic merge, we need alternating pattern
                valid_count = 0
                
                # Pattern 1: start with 0 (010101...)
                merge_0 = []
                i, j = 0, 0
                for step in range(len_x + len_y):
                    if step % 2 == 0:
                        if i < len_x:
                            merge_0.append(sub_x[i])
                            i += 1
                        else:
                            # This shouldn't happen with valid input
                            break
                    else:
                        if j < len_y:
                            merge_0.append(sub_y[j])
                            j += 1
                        else:
                            # This shouldn't happen with valid input
                            break
                
                # Check if merge_0 is chaotic
                chaotic_0 = True
                for i in range(1, len(merge_0)):
                    if merge_0[i] == merge_0[i-1]:
                        chaotic_0 = False
                        break
                
                # Pattern 2: start with 1 (101010...)
                merge_1 = []
                i, j = 0, 0
                for step in range(len_x + len_y):
                    if step % 2 == 0:
                        if j < len_y:
                            merge_1.append(sub_y[j])
                            j += 1
                        else:
                            # This shouldn't happen with valid input
                            break
                    else:
                        if i < len_x:
                            merge_1.append(sub_x[i])
                            i += 1
                        else:
                            # This shouldn't happen with valid input
                            break
                
                # Check if merge_1 is chaotic
                chaotic_1 = True
                for i in range(1, len(merge_1)):
                    if merge_1[i] == merge_1[i-1]:
                        chaotic_1 = False
                        break
                
                if chaotic_0:
                    valid_count += 1
                if chaotic_1:
                    valid_count += 1
                
                total = (total + valid_count) % MOD

print(total)
```

View Extracted Code 
MOD = 998244353

x = input().strip()
y = input().strip()

n, m = len(x), len(y)
total = 0

# For each substring of x
for l1 in range(n):
    for r1 in range(l1, n):
        # For each substring of y
        for l2 in range(m):
            for r2 in range(l2, m):
                sub_x = x[l1:r1+1]
                sub_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                
                len_x, len_y = len(sub_x), len(sub_y)
                
                # If either substring is empty, skip
                if len_x == 0 or len_y == 0:
                    continue
                
                # For chaotic merge, we need alternating pattern
                valid_count = 0
                
                # Pattern 1: start with 0 (010101...)
                merge_0 = []
                i, j = 0, 0
                for step in range(len_x + len_y):
                    if step % 2 == 0:
                        if i < len_x:
                            merge_0.append(sub_x[i])
                            i += 1
                        else:
                            # This shouldn't happen with valid input
                            break
                    else:
                        if j < len_y:
                            merge_0.append(sub_y[j])
                            j += 1
                        else:
                            # This shouldn't happen with valid input
                            break
                
                # Check if merge_0 is chaotic
                chaotic_0 = True
                for i in range(1, len(merge_0)):
                    if merge_0[i] == merge_0[i-1]:
                        chaotic_0 = False
                        break
                
                # Pattern 2: start with 1 (101010...)
                merge_1 = []
                i, j = 0, 0
                for step in range(len_x + len_y):
                    if step % 2 == 0:
                        if j < len_y:
                            merge_1.append(sub_y[j])
                            j += 1
                        else:
                            # This shouldn't happen with valid input
                            break
                    else:
                        if i < len_x:
                            merge_1.append(sub_x[i])
                            i += 1
                        else:
                            # This shouldn't happen with valid input
                            break
                
                # Check if merge_1 is chaotic
                chaotic_1 = True
                for i in range(1, len(merge_1)):
                    if merge_1[i] == merge_1[i-1]:
                        chaotic_1 = False
                        break
                
                if chaotic_0:
                    valid_count += 1
                if chaotic_1:
                    valid_count += 1
                
                total = (total + valid_count) % MOD

print(total)
